========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, I wasn't really sure if I should even come in, but... I ended up calling the Employee Assistance Program at work, and they suggested I talk to someone. I'm a sales supervisor, so I'm used to a busy office, you know? Phones ringing, people talking, the usual hustle. But lately... well, what really pushed me to make the call is how I'm reacting to just... normal noises. It's embarrassing, to be honest. Every little bang in the office—like, if someone just shuts a file drawer a little too hard, or the other day, one of the new guys dropped his coffee mug in the breakroom—I just... I jump so hard. Like, practically out of my skin. And it's not just the jumping, it's that my heart starts pounding against my ribs, and my chest just keeps racing, and it stays like that for way, way longer than it should. I try to play it off, pretend I'm just stretching or something, but it takes me forever to calm down from just a dropped mug. It's really starting to get to me, feeling this on edge all the time at work.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier at all, honestly. My whole life, I've always been kind of the... the grounded one, I guess? Like, even with work—and being a supervisor in sales, you know, there's always pressure, right? You've got quotas, you've got upper management breathing down your neck about quarterly projections, and you have to manage a team of people who are all stressed out about their own commissions... but I don't really sit at home agonizing over that stuff. I just kind of leave it at the office. 

I definitely don't worry about, you know, just random everyday things going wrong. My girlfriend actually jokes that I'm sometimes almost too relaxed about normal life stuff. Like, if the car is making a weird noise or the sink is leaking, I'm just like, 'Eh, I'll look at it this weekend,' you know? I don't lose sleep over it. I don't just sit around anticipating bad things happening out of nowhere. 

So, no, my brain isn't just spinning with worries all day. That's actually why this whole... this whole jumping out of my skin thing at the office is so incredibly frustrating to me, because it's just so not my personality. I don't have this constant loop of anxiety about my life or my future or anything like that, it's just... I'm just having these weird physical reactions out of nowhere.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really had anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? My sister used to get those when she was in high school, and it was a whole ordeal. She'd be totally fine one minute, just sitting on the couch watching TV, and the next minute she'd be gasping for air and crying, saying her chest was tight and she felt like the room was spinning. We actually had to take her to the emergency room once because my mom thought she was having a heart attack, even though she was only sixteen. It was terrifying to watch, honestly, because it really did just come out of nowhere, totally out of the blue, just like you said.

But for me? No, nothing like that. The only time I get that racing heart and that feeling in my chest is when there's a loud noise, like I was saying before. It's not out of the blue, it's always triggered by something specific. Like, if I'm just sitting at my desk typing an email and it's quiet, I don't just suddenly feel like I'm losing control or getting dizzy. I feel totally normal. It's only when someone slams a door or drops a book or something that I get that huge rush of adrenaline. And even then, I wouldn't say I feel dizzy or like I can't breathe, it's just... a massive jolt, you know? Like my body is suddenly in overdrive for no reason. But those random, out-of-nowhere episodes where you just feel terrified for no reason? No, I've never experienced anything like that myself. It's really just the reaction to the noises that's been driving me crazy lately.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I guess I haven't really had the chance to avoid much, since it mostly happens at work, and, well, I can't exactly avoid going to work, right? I'm the supervisor, so I have to be there. I can't just call in sick because I'm afraid someone might drop a stapler. 

But even outside of work, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding places. Like, my girlfriend and I went out to dinner last weekend at this place downtown—one of those trendy spots with the exposed brick and the really high ceilings where every sound echoes? Normally, that kind of place wouldn't bother me at all, but I did notice I was a little more on edge than usual, just kind of waiting for a waiter to drop a tray or something. But I still went. I didn't tell her, 'Hey, let's go somewhere quiet instead.' I just sort of dealt with it. 

I guess I'm stubborn like that. I really don't want this... whatever this is... to start dictating my life. I don't want to be the guy who can't go to a restaurant or a movie theater because he's afraid of a loud noise. That just sounds exhausting. So, yeah, even though I'm definitely more aware of my surroundings and maybe a little more tense in places where sudden noises could happen, I'm not actually avoiding them. I'm just kind of white-knuckling my way through it, which is probably why I ended up calling the EAP in the first place, because white-knuckling it every single day is getting really old, really fast. But yeah, no avoiding things, just... enduring them, I guess you could say.

Like, if I'm just sitting at my desk typing an email and it's quiet, I don't just suddenly feel like I'm losing control or getting dizzy. I feel totally normal. It's only when someone slams a door or drops a book or something that I get that huge rush of adrenaline. But those random, out-of-nowhere episodes where you just feel terrified for no reason? No, I've never experienced anything like that myself. It's really just the reaction to the noises that's been driving me crazy lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're worried about having one of these reactions again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I guess I haven't really had the chance to avoid much, since it mostly happens at work, and, well, I can't exactly avoid going to work, right? I'm the supervisor, so I have to be there. I can't just call in sick because I'm afraid someone might drop a stapler. \n\nBut even outside of work, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding places. Like, my girlfriend and I went out to dinner last weekend at this place downtown—one of those trendy spots with the exposed brick and the really high ceilings where every sound echoes? I didn't tell her, 'Hey, let's go somewhere quiet instead.' I just sort of dealt with it. \n\nI guess I'm stubborn like that. I really don't want this... whatever this is... to start dictating my life. I don't want to be the guy who can't go to a restaurant or a movie theater because he's afraid of a loud noise. That just sounds exhausting. So, yeah, even though I'm definitely more aware of my surroundings and maybe a little more tense in places where sudden noises could happen, I'm not actually avoiding them. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, outside of work... maybe a little? But honestly, it's mostly at the office where it's really, really noticeable. It's like... I don't know, it's like my brain won't turn off when I'm there. 

Like, the other day, I was supposed to be running this morning huddle with my team—just going over the weekly targets, nothing crazy. But instead of just focusing on what I was saying, I realized I was constantly... scanning the room. Like, I kept glancing over at my boss's office to see if his door was open, and then I'd check the main entrance to the sales floor, just to see who was walking in. And I caught myself repeatedly looking over my shoulder, checking if anyone was coming up behind me while I was talking. 

It's so weird, because I've been in that office for three years. I know the layout perfectly. I know all these people. But suddenly I'm tracking the exits and keeping tabs on everyone's movements like I'm a security guard or something, instead of just a sales supervisor. And it's exhausting, because I'm trying to listen to my team's updates and give them feedback, but half my brain is busy doing this weird threat assessment of the room. It’s like I can’t just sit at my desk and do my work anymore without keeping one eye on the door. So, yeah, it's definitely happening, but it's just so much worse when I'm actually in the building.

Well, like I was just saying, it's really the office where it's out of control. At home, I mean, maybe a little bit? But it's nothing compared to work. I kind of just mentioned this, but it's like I'm on high alert the second I walk through those doors. 

I was telling you about that morning huddle, right? It's just... it's so frustrating because I'm the supervisor. I'm supposed to be the one leading the meeting, keeping everyone on track, making sure we hit our numbers for the week. And instead, I'm standing there, and I literally cannot stop myself from checking where my boss is. Like, I'll be mid-sentence, talking to one of my reps about their call volume, and my eyes are just darting over to his office. And then I'm checking the exits. I'm looking at the main doors, I'm looking at the hallway that leads to the breakroom. I'm constantly checking if anyone is coming up behind me. 

It's like I have to know where everyone is and what everyone is doing at all times. And I'm supposed to be focused on my team! I'm supposed to be listening to them, but my brain is just doing this constant sweep of the room. It’s exhausting. It’s like I’m a completely different person when I’m there. At home, I can kind of decompress a little, sit on the couch, watch a game, but at the office? It’s just this non-stop radar going off in my head, tracking every movement, every person, every door. I just can't turn it off when I'm in that building.

Every little bang in the office—like, if someone just shuts a file drawer a little too hard, or the other day, one of the new guys dropped his coffee mug in the breakroom—I just... I jump so hard. Like, practically out of my skin. Like, if the car is making a weird noise or the sink is leaking, I'm just like, 'Eh, I'll look at it this weekend,' you know? I don't lose sleep over it. I don't just sit around anticipating bad things happening out of nowhere. \n\nSo, no, my brain isn't just spinning with worries all day. That's actually why this whole... this whole jumping out of my skin thing at the office is so incredibly frustrating to me, because it's just so not my personality. I don't have this constant loop of anxiety about my life or my future or anything like that, it's just... I'm just having these weird physical reactions out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that comes out of the blue, like a rush of physical symptoms such as shortness of breath, dizziness, or a feeling like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? My sister used to get those when she was in high school, and it was a whole ordeal. She'd be totally fine one minute, just sitting on the couch watching TV, and the next minute she'd be gasping for air and crying, saying her chest was tight and she felt like the room was spinning. We actually had to take her to the emergency room once because my mom thought she was having a heart attack, even though she was only sixteen. It was terrifying to watch, honestly, because it really did just come out of nowhere, totally out of the blue, just like you said.\n\nBut for me? No, nothing like that. The only time I get that racing heart and that feeling in my chest is when there's a loud noise, like I was saying before. It's not out of the blue, it's always triggered by something specific. Like, if I'm just sitting at my desk typing an email and it's quiet, I don't just suddenly feel like I'm losing control or getting dizzy. I feel totally normal. It's only when someone slams a door or drops a book or something that I get that huge rush of adrenaline. And even then, I wouldn't say I feel dizzy or like I can't breathe, it's just... a massive jolt, you know? Like my body is suddenly in overdrive for no reason. But those random, out-of-nowhere episodes where you just feel terrified for no reason? No, I've never experienced anything like that myself. It's really just the reaction to the noises that's been driving me crazy lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're worried about having one of these reactions again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I guess I haven't really had the chance to avoid much, since it mostly happens at work, and, well, I can't exactly avoid going to work, right? I'm the supervisor, so I have to be there. I can't just call in sick because I'm afraid someone might drop a stapler. \n\nBut even outside of work, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding places. Like, my girlfriend and I went out to dinner last weekend at this place downtown—one of those trendy spots with the exposed brick and the really high ceilings where every sound echoes? Normally, that kind of place wouldn't bother me at all, but I did notice I was a little more on edge than usual, just kind of waiting for a waiter to drop a tray or something. But I still went. I didn't tell her, 'Hey, let's go somewhere quiet instead.' I just sort of dealt with it. \n\nI guess I'm stubborn like that. I really don't want this... whatever this is... to start dictating my life. I don't want to be the guy who can't go to a restaurant or a movie theater because he's afraid of a loud noise. That just sounds exhausting. So, yeah, even though I'm definitely more aware of my surroundings and maybe a little more tense in places where sudden noises could happen, I'm not actually avoiding them. I'm just kind of white-knuckling my way through it, which is probably why I ended up calling the EAP in the first place, because white-knuckling it every single day is getting really old, really fast. But yeah, no avoiding things, just... enduring them, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself being unusually alert or easily startled, even in quiet or familiar environments?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of work... maybe a little? But honestly, it's mostly at the office where it's really, really noticeable. It's like... I don't know, it's like my brain won't turn off when I'm there. \n\nLike, the other day, I was supposed to be running this morning huddle with my team—just going over the weekly targets, nothing crazy. But instead of just focusing on what I was saying, I realized I was constantly... scanning the room. Like, I kept glancing over at my boss's office to see if his door was open, and then I'd check the main entrance to the sales floor, just to see who was walking in. And I caught myself repeatedly looking over my shoulder, checking if anyone was coming up behind me while I was talking. \n\nIt's so weird, because I've been in that office for three years. I know the layout perfectly. I know all these people. But suddenly I'm tracking the exits and keeping tabs on everyone's movements like I'm a security guard or something, instead of just a sales supervisor. And it's exhausting, because I'm trying to listen to my team's updates and give them feedback, but half my brain is busy doing this weird threat assessment of the room. It’s like I can’t just sit at my desk and do my work anymore without keeping one eye on the door. So, yeah, it's definitely happening, but it's just so much worse when I'm actually in the building.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself staying alert or tense even in safe, quiet places like your home, even when there’s no reason to be on edge?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, it's really the office where it's out of control. At home, I mean, maybe a little bit? But it's nothing compared to work. I kind of just mentioned this, but it's like I'm on high alert the second I walk through those doors. \n\nI was telling you about that morning huddle, right? It's just... it's so frustrating because I'm the supervisor. I'm supposed to be the one leading the meeting, keeping everyone on track, making sure we hit our numbers for the week. And instead, I'm standing there, and I literally cannot stop myself from checking where my boss is. Like, I'll be mid-sentence, talking to one of my reps about their call volume, and my eyes are just darting over to his office. And then I'm checking the exits. I'm looking at the main doors, I'm looking at the hallway that leads to the breakroom. I'm constantly checking if anyone is coming up behind me. \n\nIt's like I have to know where everyone is and what everyone is doing at all times. And I'm supposed to be focused on my team! I'm supposed to be listening to them, but my brain is just doing this constant sweep of the room. It’s exhausting. It’s like I’m a completely different person when I’m there. At home, I can kind of decompress a little, sit on the couch, watch a game, but at the office? It’s just this non-stop radar going off in my head, tracking every movement, every person, every door. I just can't turn it off when I'm in that building.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s like I can’t just sit at my desk and do my work anymore without keeping one eye on the door. So, yeah, it's definitely happening, but it's just so much worse when I'm actually in the building.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself staying alert or tense even in safe, quiet places like your home, even when there’s no reason to be on edge?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, it's really the office where it's out of control. At home, I mean, maybe a little bit? But it's nothing compared to work. I kind of just mentioned this, but it's like I'm on high alert the second I walk through those doors. \n\nI was telling you about that morning huddle, right? It's just... it's so frustrating because I'm the supervisor. I'm supposed to be the one leading the meeting, keeping everyone on track, making sure we hit our numbers for the week. And instead, I'm standing there, and I literally cannot stop myself from checking where my boss is. Like, I'll be mid-sentence, talking to one of my reps about their call volume, and my eyes are just darting over to his office. And then I'm checking the exits. I'm looking at the main doors, I'm looking at the hallway that leads to the breakroom. I'm constantly checking if anyone is coming up behind me. \n\nIt's like I have to know where everyone is and what everyone is doing at all times. And I'm supposed to be focused on my team! I'm supposed to be listening to them, but my brain is just doing this constant sweep of the room. It’s exhausting. It’s like I’m a completely different person when I’m there. At home, I can kind of decompress a little, sit on the couch, watch a game, but at the office? It’s just this non-stop radar going off in my head, tracking every movement, every person, every door. I just can't turn it off when I'm in that building.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had memories or thoughts about a past event that keep coming back and feel really upsetting or disturbing to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / first_line_supervisor_of_non_retail_sales_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Memories of a past event? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think so. It's not like I'm sitting around daydreaming about something terrible that happened years ago, if that's what you mean. 

Like, I know some people have that kind of thing, where they can't stop thinking about a car accident they were in or something like that, but for me... no. It's really just the noise thing and the feeling on edge at work, like I was saying. I don't have these, like, intrusive flashbacks to my childhood or some past trauma. My childhood was pretty normal, honestly. Grew up in the suburbs, played baseball, went to some college before I realized I was better at sales than sitting in a lecture hall. Nothing crazy or traumatic happened to me back then. 

And even recently, I mean, nothing stands out. I haven't been in any accidents, haven't been mugged, nothing like that. So, no, I don't have any upsetting memories playing on a loop in my head. It's really just... well, it's just the present moment that's the problem, you know? It's just being in that office right now, today, and feeling like my nerves are completely fried. But as for past events coming back to haunt me? No, I definitely don't have anything like that going on. I'm just trying to get through the work week without jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen.

Well, running errands or in a crowd? Um... I mean, maybe a tiny bit if a place is super loud, but honestly, like I've been trying to explain, it's really the office. I feel like a broken record here, but it's just so intense when I'm at work compared to anywhere else.

I'm looking over at my boss's glass office to see if he's on the phone or if he's looking out at us. And then my eyes drift to the main double doors to see if anyone from HR or upper management is walking in. And then I'm checking the hallway to the restrooms, and I'm constantly shifting my weight to look over my shoulder, just to make sure nobody is walking up behind me while I'm talking. It's like I have to map out every single person's location in the room, and the exits, and the blind spots, all while trying to give a cohesive speech about sales targets. It's completely draining. So, sure, maybe if I'm at a really packed grocery store I might feel a little antsy, but it's nothing—I mean nothing—like the constant radar I have going off when I'm in that office trying to manage my team.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, at home watching TV, like I said, I can usually decompress. If a door slams at my house, it's just my roommate coming home, you know? It's fine. But... well, actually, the sound thing is exactly why I finally called the Employee Assistance Program. 

Like, it's not at home, and it's not when I'm out having fun, but at work? It's ridiculous. I mean, it's beyond just checking the exits like I was talking about. It's the noises. Every single little bang or pop in that office just sends me through the roof. And it's an office! There are noises all the time. But lately, if someone just shuts a metal file drawer a little too hard—like, you know those heavy lateral cabinets that clank when they close?—if I hear that, I jump so hard I practically come out of my chair. 

Or last week, one of my newer reps, nice kid but a little clumsy, he dropped his ceramic coffee mug on the tile floor in the breakroom. It shattered, made this huge crash. Normal people just look over, right? Maybe say "oops." But me? My whole body jerked, my shoulders went up to my ears, and my heart... man, my chest just started pounding. And the worst part is, it doesn't stop. It's not just a quick startle and then I'm fine. My heart will keep racing for, like, ten or fifteen minutes after something stupid like a dropped mug. I'll be sitting there trying to review call logs, and I can literally feel my pulse in my neck, just from a loud noise. It's exhausting, honestly. It takes way longer than it should for me to calm down after a sudden sound, but only when I'm in that building. That's what really pushed me to make the call and get some help, because I can't keep jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen or shuts a drawer.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.10, F40.290, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I wasn't really sure if I should even come in, but... I ended up calling the Employee Assistance Program at work, and they suggested I talk to someone. I'm a sales supervisor, so I'm used to a busy office, you know? Phones ringing, people talking, the usual hustle. But lately... well, what really pushed me to make the call is how I'm reacting to just... normal noises. It's embarrassing, to be honest. Every little bang in the office—like, if someone just shuts a file drawer a little too hard, or the other day, one of the new guys dropped his coffee mug in the breakroom—I just... I jump so hard. Like, practically out of my skin. And it's not just the jumping, it's that my heart starts pounding against my ribs, and my chest just keeps racing, and it stays like that for way, way longer than it should. I try to play it off, pretend I'm just stretching or something, but it takes me forever to calm down from just a dropped mug. It's really starting to get to me, feeling this on edge all the time at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things in your life, even when there isn’t a specific problem happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier at all, honestly. My whole life, I've always been kind of the... the grounded one, I guess? Like, even with work—and being a supervisor in sales, you know, there's always pressure, right? You've got quotas, you've got upper management breathing down your neck about quarterly projections, and you have to manage a team of people who are all stressed out about their own commissions... but I don't really sit at home agonizing over that stuff. I just kind of leave it at the office. \n\nI definitely don't worry about, you know, just random everyday things going wrong. My girlfriend actually jokes that I'm sometimes almost too relaxed about normal life stuff. Like, if the car is making a weird noise or the sink is leaking, I'm just like, 'Eh, I'll look at it this weekend,' you know? I don't lose sleep over it. I don't just sit around anticipating bad things happening out of nowhere. \n\nSo, no, my brain isn't just spinning with worries all day. That's actually why this whole... this whole jumping out of my skin thing at the office is so incredibly frustrating to me, because it's just so not my personality. I don't have this constant loop of anxiety about my life or my future or anything like that, it's just... I'm just having these weird physical reactions out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that comes out of the blue, like a rush of physical symptoms such as shortness of breath, dizziness, or a feeling like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? My sister used to get those when she was in high school, and it was a whole ordeal. She'd be totally fine one minute, just sitting on the couch watching TV, and the next minute she'd be gasping for air and crying, saying her chest was tight and she felt like the room was spinning. We actually had to take her to the emergency room once because my mom thought she was having a heart attack, even though she was only sixteen. It was terrifying to watch, honestly, because it really did just come out of nowhere, totally out of the blue, just like you said.\n\nBut for me? No, nothing like that. The only time I get that racing heart and that feeling in my chest is when there's a loud noise, like I was saying before. It's not out of the blue, it's always triggered by something specific. Like, if I'm just sitting at my desk typing an email and it's quiet, I don't just suddenly feel like I'm losing control or getting dizzy. I feel totally normal. It's only when someone slams a door or drops a book or something that I get that huge rush of adrenaline. And even then, I wouldn't say I feel dizzy or like I can't breathe, it's just... a massive jolt, you know? Like my body is suddenly in overdrive for no reason. But those random, out-of-nowhere episodes where you just feel terrified for no reason? No, I've never experienced anything like that myself. It's really just the reaction to the noises that's been driving me crazy lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're worried about having one of these reactions again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I guess I haven't really had the chance to avoid much, since it mostly happens at work, and, well, I can't exactly avoid going to work, right? I'm the supervisor, so I have to be there. I can't just call in sick because I'm afraid someone might drop a stapler. \n\nBut even outside of work, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding places. Like, my girlfriend and I went out to dinner last weekend at this place downtown—one of those trendy spots with the exposed brick and the really high ceilings where every sound echoes? Normally, that kind of place wouldn't bother me at all, but I did notice I was a little more on edge than usual, just kind of waiting for a waiter to drop a tray or something. But I still went. I didn't tell her, 'Hey, let's go somewhere quiet instead.' I just sort of dealt with it. \n\nI guess I'm stubborn like that. I really don't want this... whatever this is... to start dictating my life. I don't want to be the guy who can't go to a restaurant or a movie theater because he's afraid of a loud noise. That just sounds exhausting. So, yeah, even though I'm definitely more aware of my surroundings and maybe a little more tense in places where sudden noises could happen, I'm not actually avoiding them. I'm just kind of white-knuckling my way through it, which is probably why I ended up calling the EAP in the first place, because white-knuckling it every single day is getting really old, really fast. But yeah, no avoiding things, just... enduring them, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself being unusually alert or easily startled, even in quiet or familiar environments?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of work... maybe a little? But honestly, it's mostly at the office where it's really, really noticeable. It's like... I don't know, it's like my brain won't turn off when I'm there. \n\nLike, the other day, I was supposed to be running this morning huddle with my team—just going over the weekly targets, nothing crazy. But instead of just focusing on what I was saying, I realized I was constantly... scanning the room. Like, I kept glancing over at my boss's office to see if his door was open, and then I'd check the main entrance to the sales floor, just to see who was walking in. And I caught myself repeatedly looking over my shoulder, checking if anyone was coming up behind me while I was talking. \n\nIt's so weird, because I've been in that office for three years. I know the layout perfectly. I know all these people. But suddenly I'm tracking the exits and keeping tabs on everyone's movements like I'm a security guard or something, instead of just a sales supervisor. And it's exhausting, because I'm trying to listen to my team's updates and give them feedback, but half my brain is busy doing this weird threat assessment of the room. It’s like I can’t just sit at my desk and do my work anymore without keeping one eye on the door. So, yeah, it's definitely happening, but it's just so much worse when I'm actually in the building.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself staying alert or tense even in safe, quiet places like your home, even when there’s no reason to be on edge?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, it's really the office where it's out of control. At home, I mean, maybe a little bit? But it's nothing compared to work. I kind of just mentioned this, but it's like I'm on high alert the second I walk through those doors. \n\nI was telling you about that morning huddle, right? It's just... it's so frustrating because I'm the supervisor. I'm supposed to be the one leading the meeting, keeping everyone on track, making sure we hit our numbers for the week. And instead, I'm standing there, and I literally cannot stop myself from checking where my boss is. Like, I'll be mid-sentence, talking to one of my reps about their call volume, and my eyes are just darting over to his office. And then I'm checking the exits. I'm looking at the main doors, I'm looking at the hallway that leads to the breakroom. I'm constantly checking if anyone is coming up behind me. \n\nIt's like I have to know where everyone is and what everyone is doing at all times. And I'm supposed to be focused on my team! I'm supposed to be listening to them, but my brain is just doing this constant sweep of the room. It’s exhausting. It’s like I’m a completely different person when I’m there. At home, I can kind of decompress a little, sit on the couch, watch a game, but at the office? It’s just this non-stop radar going off in my head, tracking every movement, every person, every door. I just can't turn it off when I'm in that building.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had memories or thoughts about a past event that keep coming back and feel really upsetting or disturbing to you?\n\nPatient: Memories of a past event? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think so. It's not like I'm sitting around daydreaming about something terrible that happened years ago, if that's what you mean. \n\nLike, I know some people have that kind of thing, where they can't stop thinking about a car accident they were in or something like that, but for me... no. It's really just the noise thing and the feeling on edge at work, like I was saying. I don't have these, like, intrusive flashbacks to my childhood or some past trauma. My childhood was pretty normal, honestly. Grew up in the suburbs, played baseball, went to some college before I realized I was better at sales than sitting in a lecture hall. Nothing crazy or traumatic happened to me back then. \n\nAnd even recently, I mean, nothing stands out. I haven't been in any accidents, haven't been mugged, nothing like that. So, no, I don't have any upsetting memories playing on a loop in my head. It's really just... well, it's just the present moment that's the problem, you know? It's just being in that office right now, today, and feeling like my nerves are completely fried. But as for past events coming back to haunt me? No, I definitely don't have anything like that going on. I'm just trying to get through the work week without jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the same kind of heightened alertness or jumpiness in other places, like when you're out running errands or in a crowd, even if you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Well, running errands or in a crowd? And then I'm checking the hallway to the restrooms, and I'm constantly shifting my weight to look over my shoulder, just to make sure nobody is walking up behind me while I'm talking. It's like I have to map out every single person's location in the room, and the exits, and the blind spots, all while trying to give a cohesive speech about sales targets. It's completely draining. So, sure, maybe if I'm at a really packed grocery store I might feel a little antsy, but it's nothing—I mean nothing—like the constant radar I have going off when I'm in that office trying to manage my team.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or relaxing, or when you're out doing something for fun, do you ever notice yourself staying really alert or reacting strongly to sudden sounds, like a door slamming or a phone ringing?\n\nPatient: Well, at home watching TV, like I said, I can usually decompress. I try to play it off, pretend I'm just stretching or something, but it takes me forever to calm down from just a dropped mug. It's really starting to get to me, feeling this on edge all the time at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things in your life, even when there isn’t a specific problem happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier at all, honestly. My whole life, I've always been kind of the... the grounded one, I guess? Like, even with work—and being a supervisor in sales, you know, there's always pressure, right? You've got quotas, you've got upper management breathing down your neck about quarterly projections, and you have to manage a team of people who are all stressed out about their own commissions... but I don't really sit at home agonizing over that stuff. I just kind of leave it at the office. \n\nI definitely don't worry about, you know, just random everyday things going wrong. My girlfriend actually jokes that I'm sometimes almost too relaxed about normal life stuff. I don't just sit around anticipating bad things happening out of nowhere. \n\nSo, no, my brain isn't just spinning with worries all day. That's actually why this whole... this whole jumping out of my skin thing at the office is so incredibly frustrating to me, because it's just so not my personality. I don't have this constant loop of anxiety about my life or my future or anything like that, it's just... I'm just having these weird physical reactions out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that comes out of the blue, like a rush of physical symptoms such as shortness of breath, dizziness, or a feeling like you're losing control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like a panic attack, right? My sister used to get those when she was in high school, and it was a whole ordeal. She'd be totally fine one minute, just sitting on the couch watching TV, and the next minute she'd be gasping for air and crying, saying her chest was tight and she felt like the room was spinning. We actually had to take her to the emergency room once because my mom thought she was having a heart attack, even though she was only sixteen. It was terrifying to watch, honestly, because it really did just come out of nowhere, totally out of the blue, just like you said.\n\nBut for me? No, nothing like that. The only time I get that racing heart and that feeling in my chest is when there's a loud noise, like I was saying before. It's not out of the blue, it's always triggered by something specific. Like, if I'm just sitting at my desk typing an email and it's quiet, I don't just suddenly feel like I'm losing control or getting dizzy. I feel totally normal. It's only when someone slams a door or drops a book or something that I get that huge rush of adrenaline. And even then, I wouldn't say I feel dizzy or like I can't breathe, it's just... a massive jolt, you know? Like my body is suddenly in overdrive for no reason. But those random, out-of-nowhere episodes where you just feel terrified for no reason? No, I've never experienced anything like that myself. It's really just the reaction to the noises that's been driving me crazy lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're worried about having one of these reactions again?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I guess I haven't really had the chance to avoid much, since it mostly happens at work, and, well, I can't exactly avoid going to work, right? I'm the supervisor, so I have to be there. I can't just call in sick because I'm afraid someone might drop a stapler. \n\nBut even outside of work, no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding places. Like, my girlfriend and I went out to dinner last weekend at this place downtown—one of those trendy spots with the exposed brick and the really high ceilings where every sound echoes? Normally, that kind of place wouldn't bother me at all, but I did notice I was a little more on edge than usual, just kind of waiting for a waiter to drop a tray or something. But I still went. I didn't tell her, 'Hey, let's go somewhere quiet instead.' I just sort of dealt with it. \n\nI guess I'm stubborn like that. I really don't want this... whatever this is... to start dictating my life. I don't want to be the guy who can't go to a restaurant or a movie theater because he's afraid of a loud noise. That just sounds exhausting. So, yeah, even though I'm definitely more aware of my surroundings and maybe a little more tense in places where sudden noises could happen, I'm not actually avoiding them. I'm just kind of white-knuckling my way through it, which is probably why I ended up calling the EAP in the first place, because white-knuckling it every single day is getting really old, really fast. But yeah, no avoiding things, just... enduring them, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself being unusually alert or easily startled, even in quiet or familiar environments?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of work... maybe a little? But honestly, it's mostly at the office where it's really, really noticeable. It's like... I don't know, it's like my brain won't turn off when I'm there. \n\nLike, the other day, I was supposed to be running this morning huddle with my team—just going over the weekly targets, nothing crazy. But instead of just focusing on what I was saying, I realized I was constantly... scanning the room. Like, I kept glancing over at my boss's office to see if his door was open, and then I'd check the main entrance to the sales floor, just to see who was walking in. And I caught myself repeatedly looking over my shoulder, checking if anyone was coming up behind me while I was talking. \n\nIt's so weird, because I've been in that office for three years. I know the layout perfectly. I know all these people. But suddenly I'm tracking the exits and keeping tabs on everyone's movements like I'm a security guard or something, instead of just a sales supervisor. And it's exhausting, because I'm trying to listen to my team's updates and give them feedback, but half my brain is busy doing this weird threat assessment of the room. It’s like I can’t just sit at my desk and do my work anymore without keeping one eye on the door. So, yeah, it's definitely happening, but it's just so much worse when I'm actually in the building.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself staying alert or tense even in safe, quiet places like your home, even when there’s no reason to be on edge?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, it's really the office where it's out of control. At home, I mean, maybe a little bit? But it's nothing compared to work. I kind of just mentioned this, but it's like I'm on high alert the second I walk through those doors. \n\nI was telling you about that morning huddle, right? It's just... it's so frustrating because I'm the supervisor. I'm supposed to be the one leading the meeting, keeping everyone on track, making sure we hit our numbers for the week. And instead, I'm standing there, and I literally cannot stop myself from checking where my boss is. Like, I'll be mid-sentence, talking to one of my reps about their call volume, and my eyes are just darting over to his office. And then I'm checking the exits. I'm looking at the main doors, I'm looking at the hallway that leads to the breakroom. I'm constantly checking if anyone is coming up behind me. \n\nIt's like I have to know where everyone is and what everyone is doing at all times. And I'm supposed to be focused on my team! I'm supposed to be listening to them, but my brain is just doing this constant sweep of the room. It’s exhausting. It’s like I’m a completely different person when I’m there. At home, I can kind of decompress a little, sit on the couch, watch a game, but at the office? It’s just this non-stop radar going off in my head, tracking every movement, every person, every door. I just can't turn it off when I'm in that building.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had memories or thoughts about a past event that keep coming back and feel really upsetting or disturbing to you?\n\nPatient: Memories of a past event? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think so. It's not like I'm sitting around daydreaming about something terrible that happened years ago, if that's what you mean. \n\nLike, I know some people have that kind of thing, where they can't stop thinking about a car accident they were in or something like that, but for me... no. It's really just the noise thing and the feeling on edge at work, like I was saying. I don't have these, like, intrusive flashbacks to my childhood or some past trauma. My childhood was pretty normal, honestly. Grew up in the suburbs, played baseball, went to some college before I realized I was better at sales than sitting in a lecture hall. Nothing crazy or traumatic happened to me back then. \n\nAnd even recently, I mean, nothing stands out. I haven't been in any accidents, haven't been mugged, nothing like that. So, no, I don't have any upsetting memories playing on a loop in my head. It's really just... well, it's just the present moment that's the problem, you know? It's just being in that office right now, today, and feeling like my nerves are completely fried. But as for past events coming back to haunt me? No, I definitely don't have anything like that going on. I'm just trying to get through the work week without jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the same kind of heightened alertness or jumpiness in other places, like when you're out running errands or in a crowd, even if you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Well, running errands or in a crowd? Um... I mean, maybe a tiny bit if a place is super loud, but honestly, like I've been trying to explain, it's really the office. I feel like a broken record here, but it's just so intense when I'm at work compared to anywhere else. \n\nI know you're asking about other places, but I just keep thinking about what it's like when I'm actually on the clock. Like, I was just saying about that morning huddle... it's just this constant, exhausting loop. I'm standing there, right? I've got my clipboard, I'm looking at my team, I'm supposed to be going over the call metrics from the day before—which were actually pretty good, we had a 15% bump in outbound connects, which I was proud of—but instead of focusing on praising my guys for that, I'm just... scanning. \n\nI'm looking over at my boss's glass office to see if he's on the phone or if he's looking out at us. So, sure, maybe if I'm at a really packed grocery store I might feel a little antsy, but it's nothing—I mean nothing—like the constant radar I have going off when I'm in that office trying to manage my team.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or relaxing, or when you're out doing something for fun, do you ever notice yourself staying really alert or reacting strongly to sudden sounds, like a door slamming or a phone ringing?\n\nPatient: Well, at home watching TV, like I said, I can usually decompress. It shattered, made this huge crash. Normal people just look over, right? Maybe say \"oops.\" But me? My heart will keep racing for, like, ten or fifteen minutes after something stupid like a dropped mug. I'll be sitting there trying to review call logs, and I can literally feel my pulse in my neck, just from a loud noise. It's exhausting, honestly. It takes way longer than it should for me to calm down after a sudden sound, but only when I'm in that building. That's what really pushed me to make the call and get some help, because I can't keep jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen or shuts a drawer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself reacting strongly to sudden sounds even when you're not at work, like at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I know you're asking about home again, but like I was just saying, it's really the office that's the problem. And then I'm checking the hallway to the restrooms, and I'm constantly shifting my weight to look over my shoulder, just to make sure nobody is walking up behind me while I'm talking. It's like I have to map out every single person's location in the room, and the exits, and the blind spots, all while trying to give a cohesive speech about sales targets. It's completely draining. So, sure, maybe if I'm at a really packed grocery store I might feel a little antsy, but it's nothing—I mean nothing—like the constant radar I have going off when I'm in that office trying to manage my team.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or relaxing, or when you're out doing something for fun, do you ever notice yourself staying really alert or reacting strongly to sudden sounds, like a door slamming or a phone ringing?\n\nPatient: Well, at home watching TV, like I said, I can usually decompress. If a door slams at my house, it's just my roommate coming home, you know? It's fine. I mean, it's beyond just checking the exits like I was talking about. It's the noises. Every single little bang or pop in that office just sends me through the roof. And it's an office! There are noises all the time. It shattered, made this huge crash. Normal people just look over, right? Maybe say \"oops.\" But me? My whole body jerked, my shoulders went up to my ears, and my heart... man, my chest just started pounding. And the worst part is, it doesn't stop. It's not just a quick startle and then I'm fine. My heart will keep racing for, like, ten or fifteen minutes after something stupid like a dropped mug. I'll be sitting there trying to review call logs, and I can literally feel my pulse in my neck, just from a loud noise. It's exhausting, honestly. It takes way longer than it should for me to calm down after a sudden sound, but only when I'm in that building. That's what really pushed me to make the call and get some help, because I can't keep jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen or shuts a drawer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself reacting strongly to sudden sounds even when you're not at work, like at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I know you're asking about home again, but like I was just saying, it's really the office that's the problem. It's happening at work. \n\nThat's the whole reason I called the EAP in the first place, you know? The Employee Assistance Program? Because I just couldn't take it anymore at the office. It's the noises there. Like I was just describing with the coffee mug and the file cabinets. Every little bang or clank in that building just completely sets me off. I jump so hard, like, physically jerk in my chair, and then my chest is just pounding and racing for way, way longer than it should. Doc, I... I really don't mean to be difficult here, but I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining myself right, or maybe we're just completely missing each other on this. Because you keep asking about home, and I honestly don't know how else to say it. No. Not at home. Not in my free time. If I'm at home and a door slams, I'm fine. It's every little bang in the office.

And then my chest... it just keeps racing. It races for way, way longer than it ever should. It's not a normal 'oh, you startled me' reaction. It's like my heart is trying to beat its way out of my ribcage for ten, fifteen minutes after someone just drops a mug. I... well, no. Not really. I mean, I don't think so? I haven't been in a war or a car crash or anything like that, if that's what you mean. Well, outside of work, like I've been saying, things are mostly okay. I mean, maybe I'm a little more tired because work is draining me, but I'm not snapping at my roommate or tossing and turning all night. My sleep is fine. It's really just the office that's getting to me.

I know where the doors are. But I still look. And I hate having people walk behind me now. My desk is sort of near a main walkway, and every time someone walks past, I have to turn and look. I just can't focus on my team or the metrics because I'm so preoccupied with tracking every single person's movement in the room and making sure nobody is sneaking up on me. It's exhausting, honestly, just being that keyed up and watchful the whole nine hours I'm there. But again, the second I clock out and go home, that stops. I don't care who walks behind me at the grocery store. It's just when I'm in that office.

I know we keep going back to this, and I feel like a broken record, but no. Not at home, not out with friends. I really feel like I'm just not getting this across right, because it's strictly an office thing.

That's what I need help with, you know? How to stop jumping out of my skin every time someone in the breakroom is a little too loud. Because at home, I'm fine. It's just the office.\n\nDoctor: Even if not as intense as at work, have you ever noticed yourself having a stronger physical reaction to sudden sounds—like a quicker heartbeat or muscle tension—when you're not at the office, such as at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I really don't mean to be difficult here, but I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining myself right, or maybe we're just completely missing each other on this. Because you keep asking about home, and I honestly don't know how else to say it. No. Not at home. Not in my free time. If I'm at home and a door slams, I'm fine. My muscles don't tense up, my heart doesn't beat faster. I'm just sitting on my couch. \n\nBut at work... and I know I keep repeating myself, but this is the whole crux of the issue. This is literally the exact reason I finally broke down and called the EAP in the first place. I was staring at that stupid laminated EAP flyer on the breakroom bulletin board for like a week before I actually dialed the number, because I just couldn't handle it anymore. It's every little bang in the office. \n\nIf someone shuts a file drawer—you know those heavy metal ones that just clatter?—or like I mentioned with my rep dropping that coffee mug... I jump so hard. I physically jump out of my seat. And then my chest... it just keeps racing. It races for way, way longer than it ever should. It's not a normal 'oh, you startled me' reaction. It's like my heart is trying to beat its way out of my ribcage for ten, fifteen minutes after someone just drops a mug. I'll be sitting there at my desk, trying to look at my team's outbound call metrics, and I can't even focus because my chest is pounding so hard just from a drawer shutting. So, no, it's not happening at home. It's the office. That's the environment where I'm jumping at every little bang, and that's exactly what drove me to get help.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been exposed to a stressful or traumatic event where you felt intense fear, helplessness, or horror—something that might explain why you’re reacting so strongly to sudden noises now?\n\nPatient: I... well, no. Not really. I mean, I don't think so? I haven't been in a war or a car crash or anything like that, if that's what you mean. I've just been living my life, going to work, managing my team... it's just a normal sales floor, you know? We do outbound B2B sales, mostly software solutions, so it's a lot of dialing, a lot of rejection, sure, but it's not dangerous. It's just stressful in a normal, corporate way. Hitting quotas, dealing with upper management getting on my case if our conversion rates drop... that kind of thing. \n\nI've been trying to rack my brain about this too, honestly, because it doesn't make sense to me either. I've been working late, coming in early, trying to keep everyone motivated when half of them are worried about getting laid off. It's a lot of pressure, and I've been feeling pretty burnt out. But that's just normal work stress, right? That's not \"intense fear, helplessness, or horror.\" It's just a headache. So, no, I haven't been through anything traumatic like what you're talking about. I'm just a guy managing a sales team who can't handle the sound of a dropped coffee mug anymore, and I don't know why.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in how you feel or how your body reacts in situations outside of work — even small ones — like feeling more irritable, having trouble sleeping, or being more tense than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of work, like I've been saying, things are mostly okay. I mean, maybe I'm a little more tired because work is draining me, but I'm not snapping at my roommate or tossing and turning all night. My sleep is fine. It's really just the office that's getting to me. \n\nBut... well, since you're asking about feeling tense or how my body reacts, there is this other thing that's been happening at work, aside from the noise stuff. I'll be sitting there, supposed to be reviewing call logs or listening in on a rep's pitch, and instead, I'm just scanning the room. I keep checking to see where my boss is—like, is he coming out of his office? Is he walking down the aisle toward my pod? \n\nAnd it's not even just him. I catch myself looking at the exits, like I need to know exactly how to get out of the room at any given second, which is ridiculous because I've worked on this floor for three years. I know where the doors are. But I still look. And I hate having people walk behind me now. My desk is sort of near a main walkway, and every time someone walks past, I have to turn and look. I just can't focus on my team or the metrics because I'm so preoccupied with tracking every single person's movement in the room and making sure nobody is sneaking up on me. It's exhausting, honestly, just being that keyed up and watchful the whole nine hours I'm there. But again, the second I clock out and go home, that stops. I don't care who walks behind me at the grocery store. It's just when I'm in that office.\n\nDoctor: Even in small ways, do you ever notice yourself being more watchful or easily startled by sudden movements — not just sounds — when you're not at work, like when you're out with friends or at home?\n\nPatient: I know we keep going back to this, and I feel like a broken record, but no. But at work... it's like my radar is just stuck on overdrive. \n\nI keep checking where my boss is, tracking him like a hawk to see if he's in his office or on the floor. I'm constantly eyeing where the exits are, which makes zero sense, but I do it anyway. And I'm obsessively checking whether anyone is coming up behind me. I'm supposed to be focused on my team—I have fifteen reps relying on me to coach them, listen to their calls, and approve their deals—but I'm wasting all this mental energy just spinning around in my chair every time a shadow moves in my peripheral vision. It's strictly in that building, nowhere else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My childhood was pretty normal, honestly. Grew up in the suburbs, played baseball, went to some college before I realized I was better at sales than sitting in a lecture hall. Nothing crazy or traumatic happened to me back then. \n\nAnd even recently, I mean, nothing stands out. I haven't been in any accidents, haven't been mugged, nothing like that. So, no, I don't have any upsetting memories playing on a loop in my head. It's really just... well, it's just the present moment that's the problem, you know? It's just being in that office right now, today, and feeling like my nerves are completely fried. But as for past events coming back to haunt me? No, I definitely don't have anything like that going on. I'm just trying to get through the work week without jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the same kind of heightened alertness or jumpiness in other places, like when you're out running errands or in a crowd, even if you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Well, running errands or in a crowd? Um... I mean, maybe a tiny bit if a place is super loud, but honestly, like I've been trying to explain, it's really the office. I feel like a broken record here, but it's just so intense when I'm at work compared to anywhere else. \n\nI know you're asking about other places, but I just keep thinking about what it's like when I'm actually on the clock. Like, I was just saying about that morning huddle... it's just this constant, exhausting loop. I'm standing there, right? I've got my clipboard, I'm looking at my team, I'm supposed to be going over the call metrics from the day before—which were actually pretty good, we had a 15% bump in outbound connects, which I was proud of—but instead of focusing on praising my guys for that, I'm just... scanning. \n\nI'm looking over at my boss's glass office to see if he's on the phone or if he's looking out at us. And then my eyes drift to the main double doors to see if anyone from HR or upper management is walking in. And then I'm checking the hallway to the restrooms, and I'm constantly shifting my weight to look over my shoulder, just to make sure nobody is walking up behind me while I'm talking. It's like I have to map out every single person's location in the room, and the exits, and the blind spots, all while trying to give a cohesive speech about sales targets. It's completely draining. So, sure, maybe if I'm at a really packed grocery store I might feel a little antsy, but it's nothing—I mean nothing—like the constant radar I have going off when I'm in that office trying to manage my team.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home watching TV or relaxing, or when you're out doing something for fun, do you ever notice yourself staying really alert or reacting strongly to sudden sounds, like a door slamming or a phone ringing?\n\nPatient: Well, at home watching TV, like I said, I can usually decompress. If a door slams at my house, it's just my roommate coming home, you know? It's fine. But... well, actually, the sound thing is exactly why I finally called the Employee Assistance Program. \n\nLike, it's not at home, and it's not when I'm out having fun, but at work? It's ridiculous. I mean, it's beyond just checking the exits like I was talking about. It's the noises. Every single little bang or pop in that office just sends me through the roof. And it's an office! There are noises all the time. But lately, if someone just shuts a metal file drawer a little too hard—like, you know those heavy lateral cabinets that clank when they close?—if I hear that, I jump so hard I practically come out of my chair. \n\nOr last week, one of my newer reps, nice kid but a little clumsy, he dropped his ceramic coffee mug on the tile floor in the breakroom. It shattered, made this huge crash. Normal people just look over, right? Maybe say \"oops.\" But me? My whole body jerked, my shoulders went up to my ears, and my heart... man, my chest just started pounding. And the worst part is, it doesn't stop. It's not just a quick startle and then I'm fine. My heart will keep racing for, like, ten or fifteen minutes after something stupid like a dropped mug. I'll be sitting there trying to review call logs, and I can literally feel my pulse in my neck, just from a loud noise. It's exhausting, honestly. It takes way longer than it should for me to calm down after a sudden sound, but only when I'm in that building. That's what really pushed me to make the call and get some help, because I can't keep jumping out of my skin every time someone drops a pen or shuts a drawer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself reacting strongly to sudden sounds even when you're not at work, like at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: I know you're asking about home again, but like I was just saying, it's really the office that's the problem. I mean, I feel like we're going in circles here a little bit, because I'm trying to tell you exactly what's going on, and it's not happening when I'm watching TV on my couch. It's happening at work. \n\nThat's the whole reason I called the EAP in the first place, you know? The Employee Assistance Program? Because I just couldn't take it anymore at the office. It's the noises there. Like I was just describing with the coffee mug and the file cabinets. Every little bang or clank in that building just completely sets me off. I jump so hard, like, physically jerk in my chair, and then my chest is just pounding and racing for way, way longer than it should. It takes me forever to calm down from something as stupid as a drawer shutting. \n\nAt home, if my roommate drops something, whatever. I might look up, but I'm not having a heart attack over it. But at work? It's like my body is just waiting for the next loud noise so it can freak out again. I'm just so on edge there, and the way I react to those sudden sounds... it's just not normal for me. That's what I need help with, you know? How to stop jumping out of my skin every time someone in the breakroom is a little too loud. Because at home, I'm fine. It's just the office.\n\nDoctor: Even if not as intense as at work, have you ever noticed yourself having a stronger physical reaction to sudden sounds—like a quicker heartbeat or muscle tension—when you're not at the office, such as at home or during your free time?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I really don't mean to be difficult here, but I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining myself right, or maybe we're just completely missing each other on this. Because you keep asking about home, and I honestly don't know how else to say it. No. Not at home. Not in my free time. If I'm at home and a door slams, I'm fine. My muscles don't tense up, my heart doesn't beat faster. I'm just sitting on my couch. \n\nBut at work... and I know I keep repeating myself, but this is the whole crux of the issue. This is literally the exact reason I finally broke down and called the EAP in the first place. I was staring at that stupid laminated EAP flyer on the breakroom bulletin board for like a week before I actually dialed the number, because I just couldn't handle it anymore. It's every little bang in the office. \n\nIf someone shuts a file drawer—you know those heavy metal ones that just clatter?—or like I mentioned with my rep dropping that coffee mug... I jump so hard. I physically jump out of my seat. And then my chest... it just keeps racing. It races for way, way longer than it ever should. It's not a normal 'oh, you startled me' reaction. It's like my heart is trying to beat its way out of my ribcage for ten, fifteen minutes after someone just drops a mug. I'll be sitting there at my desk, trying to look at my team's outbound call metrics, and I can't even focus because my chest is pounding so hard just from a drawer shutting. So, no, it's not happening at home. It's the office. That's the environment where I'm jumping at every little bang, and that's exactly what drove me to get help.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been exposed to a stressful or traumatic event where you felt intense fear, helplessness, or horror—something that might explain why you’re reacting so strongly to sudden noises now?\n\nPatient: I... well, no. Not really. I mean, I don't think so? I haven't been in a war or a car crash or anything like that, if that's what you mean. I've just been living my life, going to work, managing my team... it's just a normal sales floor, you know? We do outbound B2B sales, mostly software solutions, so it's a lot of dialing, a lot of rejection, sure, but it's not dangerous. It's just stressful in a normal, corporate way. Hitting quotas, dealing with upper management getting on my case if our conversion rates drop... that kind of thing. \n\nI've been trying to rack my brain about this too, honestly, because it doesn't make sense to me either. Why am I suddenly jumping out of my skin at the sound of a file cabinet? It's not like a file cabinet ever attacked me. But as far as some big, horrible event where I thought I was going to die or something? No. Nothing like that. \n\nThe only thing I can think of is just... I don't know, the pressure? We had a big restructuring a couple of months ago, and they merged my team with another one, so now I'm managing fifteen reps instead of eight, and the targets are just insane. I've been working late, coming in early, trying to keep everyone motivated when half of them are worried about getting laid off. It's a lot of pressure, and I've been feeling pretty burnt out. But that's just normal work stress, right? That's not \"intense fear, helplessness, or horror.\" It's just a headache. So, no, I haven't been through anything traumatic like what you're talking about. I'm just a guy managing a sales team who can't handle the sound of a dropped coffee mug anymore, and I don't know why.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in how you feel or how your body reacts in situations outside of work — even small ones — like feeling more irritable, having trouble sleeping, or being more tense than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of work, like I've been saying, things are mostly okay. I mean, maybe I'm a little more tired because work is draining me, but I'm not snapping at my roommate or tossing and turning all night. My sleep is fine. It's really just the office that's getting to me. \n\nBut... well, since you're asking about feeling tense or how my body reacts, there is this other thing that's been happening at work, aside from the noise stuff. It's like I can't just sit at my desk and do my job anymore. I'm constantly, and I mean constantly, looking around. I'll be sitting there, supposed to be reviewing call logs or listening in on a rep's pitch, and instead, I'm just scanning the room. I keep checking to see where my boss is—like, is he coming out of his office? Is he walking down the aisle toward my pod? \n\nAnd it's not even just him. I catch myself looking at the exits, like I need to know exactly how to get out of the room at any given second, which is ridiculous because I've worked on this floor for three years. I know where the doors are. But I still look. And I hate having people walk behind me now. My desk is sort of near a main walkway, and every time someone walks past, I have to turn and look. I just can't focus on my team or the metrics because I'm so preoccupied with tracking every single person's movement in the room and making sure nobody is sneaking up on me. It's exhausting, honestly, just being that keyed up and watchful the whole nine hours I'm there. But again, the second I clock out and go home, that stops. I don't care who walks behind me at the grocery store. It's just when I'm in that office.\n\nDoctor: Even in small ways, do you ever notice yourself being more watchful or easily startled by sudden movements — not just sounds — when you're not at work, like when you're out with friends or at home?\n\nPatient: I know we keep going back to this, and I feel like a broken record, but no. I'm supposed to be focused on my team—I have fifteen reps relying on me to coach them, listen to their calls, and approve their deals—but I'm wasting all this mental energy just spinning around in my chair every time a shadow moves in my peripheral vision. It's strictly in that building, nowhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had moments outside of work—like when you're at home or doing something relaxing—where just thinking about work makes your chest tighten or your heart race?\n\nPatient: I mean, no, not really.
